People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

The patient's presentation is inconsistent with bipolar disorder: (1) No clear depressive episodes—the 'flat' weeks lacked depressive mood, just anhedonia and low motivation; (2) Psychotic symptoms (voices, delusions of reference/surveillance) preceded and were independent of mood changes, persisting across all mood states; (3) The 'energized' periods lack the classic features of mania (decreased need for sleep, grandiosity, racing thoughts, increased goal-directed activity)—instead showing irritability, paranoid ideation, and voices; (4) Insidious onset over 10 months post-retirement with gradual worsening, not episodic mood cycling. And after those flat weeks ended, did the energized, driven feeling come back?\n\nPatient: I... I suppose. Most of the time since I retired, it's been... well, mostly those driven times. Feeling chosen. Irritable, sometimes. I just... I couldn't slow down for months at a time. Even if there were a few quieter days mixed in... the driven feeling is what takes up most of the time. It always comes back.\n\nDoctor: When those flat, empty weeks ended and you started feeling driven and energized again—did that shift happen over a day or two, or did it take longer to build back up?\n\nPatient: I... I'm not really sure. I don't keep track of the days like that. It just... changes. I don't really know how fast.\n\nDoctor: Before you retired ten months ago, did you ever have periods—lasting weeks or more—where you felt empty, flat, and lost interest in things, the way you described feeling at the kitchen table?\n\nPatient: I... I don't really remember. Work was just... work. I wasn't paying attention to that kind of thing back then.\n\nDoctor: Since you retired ten months ago, when you think about the whole time period—has the energized, driven feeling and the voices been pretty much constantly there together, or have they clearly separated into different times—like periods when you had the energy and plans without the voices, and other periods when you had the voices without the energy?\n\nPatient: I... they were mostly together, I think. When I was barely sleeping... just rushing around with all those archive tasks... I could hear the women talking to me. And I knew... I just knew the museum board was watching everything I did. It was all happening at once.\n\nDoctor: When you first started hearing the women's voices whispering about eight months ago—did that happen while you were already feeling energized and driven, or did the voices start first and then the energized feeling came later?\n\nPatient: I... I think the voices were there first. There was a time... maybe a few weeks... where I wasn't doing all the rushing around yet. I wasn't feeling that energy. But I could still hear the whispering in the apartment. And... I already knew the arts people were listening to my phone. It was just... quieter, I guess. Before the rest of it started."
